From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... what brings me in is I just keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I didn’t wake up. My wife, she's been so busy lately, bless her. She's putting together this big scrapbook for the family. Lots of old photos. We had to go buy special acid-free paper for it down at that craft place on Main Street. \n\nYou know, that building used to be a hardware store back in the eighties. I bought my favorite set of wrenches there. You just can't find tools made like that anymore, all heavy steel. Everything is plastic now... but, uh, what was I saying? \n\nAnyway. Lately I’ve just been saying I don’t need to see another winter. The winters just... well. I remember the blizzard of '78, now that was a real winter. We were snowed in for a week straight, had to play cards by candlelight...\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or taking your own life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just trying to say... I don't know about actually doing something to myself, but I just keep thinking they'd all be so much better off if I just didn't wake up. I really mean it when I tell them I don't need to see another winter. \n\nThe cold just... it just gets right into your joints anyway. We used to have this old cast-iron radiator in the house I grew up in. You'd stand next to it and it would practically burn your leg if you weren't careful. My mother used to dry our mittens on it after we'd been out sledding. She knitted them herself, you know. Real thick wool. You can't find wool like that anymore, everything you buy at the store now is synthetic. Makes your hands sweat but doesn't actually keep them warm. I bought a pair of gloves last year at the department store, the one out by the new highway bypass... they fell apart in two weeks. Just terrible stitching.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe your mood most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: My mood? Oh, it's just... heavy. Down, I suppose. Most days I just sit in my chair by the window and watch the cars go by. It's a nice chair, really. Leather. I bought it back in 1995 from that furniture place that used to be on the corner of Elm and 4th. They tore it down a few years ago to build a bank. Like we need another bank in this town. \n\nI remember the salesman, a young fellow named Jimmy. He had this terrible comb-over, bless his heart, but he knew his upholstery. He told me that leather would outlast me, and I suppose he was right. I used to read the morning paper in that chair every single day. The paper used to be so thick, you know? Full of real news. Now it's just a few pages of advertisements and they charge you twice as much for it. I stopped getting it delivered last spring. The boy who threw it always landed it in the rosebushes anyway. My wife's prize-winning roses... she won a blue ribbon for them at the county fair back in '02...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? No... no, I can't say that I do. I used to look forward to so much, you know. Playing bridge, tending to the garden, helping out with the cooking down at the church... oh, and going to the Cubs games. I used to just love the Cubs. We'd take the train right up to Wrigley. \n\nYou know, they don't make hot dogs like they used to at that ballpark. Used to be real beef with a snappy casing. Now it's just mush. And the mustard! I don't know why they changed the mustard. It used to be this bright yellow, tangy stuff. My buddy Frank, he'd always get that mustard all over his shirt. Frank passed away back in '08... or was it '09? He had this old Ford Taurus, ran like an absolute top until the transmission finally went out on him right in the middle of the intersection at Pine and 5th. The tow truck took three hours to get there...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh. Right. No, nowadays I just sit in that leather chair I told you about. I can't even make myself care about the Cubs or the garden or any of it anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you do sit in your chair and watch the cars go by?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no pleasure. It's just... something to do, I guess. Watching them go by. You see a lot of the same cars every day at the same time. There's this one fellow who drives a blue Chevy pickup, must be a late 90s model. He goes past every afternoon right at 3:15. \n\nYou know, they built those trucks tough back then. My brother-in-law, Artie, he had a Chevy. Drove it all the way to Yellowstone and back in the summer of '98. Said it never gave him a lick of trouble the whole way, not even over the mountains. The mountains are beautiful out there, you know. We took a trip out west when the kids were still small. Stopped at Mount Rushmore. It's smaller than you think it's going to be, looking at the pictures. And the gift shop... well, they just rob you blind in those places. Five dollars for a little plastic keychain. I told my son, I said, 'We're not paying five dollars for a piece of plastic.' He threw an absolute fit, right there in the store. Kids today, they don't understand the value of a dollar. \n\nBut... no. Sitting in the chair isn't pleasurable. It's just... sitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed. I don't step on the scale much anymore. We used to have this old mechanical scale in the bathroom, the kind with the little dial that spins around. My wife bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, it must have been '85. It was green. Avocado green, they called it back then. Everything in the 70s and 80s was avocado green or harvest gold. Our whole kitchen was harvest gold, right down to the toaster. \n\nI remember when the toaster finally gave out. It was a Sunday morning, and I was trying to make some rye toast to go with my eggs. Just popped right and sparked, nearly scared the cat half to death. We had this big orange tabby named Marmalade. Sweetest cat you ever met, but dumb as a box of rocks. He used to try and catch the dust motes in the sunlight... \n\nBut, uh, no... I don't think I've lost much weight. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh... yes. Yes, I do. All the time now. I just sit in that chair and I keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With my son, with my family... I feel like I'm just no good to anybody anymore. I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me, I really do. \n\nI was thinking about it just the other day, how I used to discipline the boy. We had this old leather belt, hung right on the back of the pantry door. The pantry had these squeaky hinges, drove my wife absolutely up the wall. I always told her I'd get around to oiling them, but you know how it is. You get busy with work, and the yard needs mowing, and the gutters need cleaning out. I used to clean my own gutters right up until I turned 70. Had this aluminum ladder, heavy as sin. Bought it at the Sears hardware store that used to be over by the old mall. \n\nThey tore that mall down, you know. Put up one of those big outdoor shopping centers. It's just not the same. In the wintertime, you freeze half to death just trying to walk from the shoe store to the food court. The old mall had this fountain right in the middle, and people would throw pennies in it. I wonder what they did with all those pennies when they demolished the place. Probably thousands of dollars just sitting in the bottom of that fountain...\n\nBut, yes. The guilt. It's heavy. I just... I'm just no good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, my wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow these days. And speaking of snow, boy, we used to get real winters back in the day. You remember the blizzard of '78? Now that was deep snow. We were snowed in for three solid days. I had this big two-stage Toro snowblower, heavy red metal housing. They don't make them like that anymore, everything is cheap plastic. You hit one little chunk of ice and the sheer pin snaps right in half. I used to buy those pins by the dozen down at Miller's Hardware. Bob Miller ran the place. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost it in Korea, or maybe it was a hunting accident, I can't quite recall. I always wondered if he took it out at night and put it in a glass of water on the nightstand, the way my grandfather used to do with his false teeth. Granddad had this awful set of dentures that clicked every time he chewed his food... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh. Yes. It just takes me so long now. Even just getting dressed, or crossing the kitchen... it takes so long that I usually just give up before I’ve done much of anything. I just sit back down in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find any activities that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No... no, not anymore. I used to. I used to love playing bridge with the Hendersons on Tuesday nights. We had this folding card table, covered in green felt. Real felt, mind you, not that cheap synthetic stuff they use now that pills up after two games. I bought it at a sporting goods store downtown that went out of business back in '92. The owner, fellow named Arthur, he used to play minor league baseball. Had this incredible collection of signed baseballs in a glass case right by the register. I always meant to ask him if he ever played against anyone famous, but you know how it is, you just never get around to asking the questions you want to ask. \n\nLike with my tomatoes. I used to love getting out in the garden. I'd grow these big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. The trick is to put crushed eggshells in the soil, for the calcium. My mother taught me that. She used to save all her eggshells in this little ceramic chicken on the windowsill. It had a chipped beak from when my brother knocked it over with a broom handle trying to swat a fly. He was always doing things like that, my brother. Clumsy as a newborn calf. He went into accounting, which was probably for the best, sitting at a desk where he couldn't break too much. \n\nI used to cook for the church potlucks, too, and go down to Wrigley Field for the Cubs games... but now? Now I just sit in my chair. I just sit there and I can't make myself care about any of it. None of it matters anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any pleasure or enjoyment from the things you do?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Oh... no. I don't think I've felt that in a long time. Like I was just saying about the bridge games, and the garden, and the cooking for the church... and those Cubs games. I used to get such a kick out of going down to Wrigley. The smell of the roasted peanuts, you know? They used to sell them in these little brown paper bags. The bags always had grease stains on the bottom. My father used to say that's how you knew they were good. He worked at a paper mill up in Wisconsin for thirty years. The smell of that mill was something awful, like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. We lived about two miles downwind, and on humid days, you couldn't even hang the laundry out to dry because the smell would get right into the bedsheets. My mother tried using lavender water in the wash, but it just made it smell like flowers and rotten eggs. She bought the lavender from this little apothecary shop downtown... \n\nBut, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I could get myself to go to a game or play cards, I wouldn't feel anything. I just sit in my chair, like I said. I just can't make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Hmm... no, I haven't really noticed much change. My wife usually keeps track of that sort of thing. She bought this new digital scale for the bathroom. It talks to you, can you believe that? You step on it and this robotic voice announces your weight to the whole house. I told her I liked the old dial one better, the one with the little red needle. We had that scale since we moved into the house in '68. We bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place for everything. You could buy a whole house from their catalog back in the day, they'd ship it to you on a train in pieces. My uncle Arthur built one of those kit houses out near Peoria. Took him two summers to finish it. He was a carpenter, lost his left index finger to a table saw when he was twenty-five... \n\nBut, uh, weight. No, I don't think it's changed. I don't eat much anymore anyway, but my clothes seem to fit about the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay still or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Slow motion... yes. My wife says I move like I'm walking through deep snow. Every little thing, even just getting dressed in the morning, it takes me so long. I usually just give up before I've done much of anything. Crossing the kitchen feels like a marathon. We put down that new linoleum in the kitchen back in... oh, must have been '98. It has this pattern that looks like little blue cobblestones. The fellow who installed it, he left his thermos on the counter. One of those heavy, metal Stanley thermoses. The green ones, you know? They don't make them like that anymore. Now they're all plastic and they don't keep the coffee hot for more than an hour. My father-in-law used to take a Stanley thermos with him to the railyard every single day. He worked for the Union Pacific. He used to tell these stories about the trains, the big diesel engines... he said the noise would shake your bones right inside your skin... \n\nBut, yes, everything just feels so slow. I can't seem to get my body to move the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleeping... well, I suppose I spend a lot of time in the chair. But sleep? No, I don't think I'm actually sleeping more. It's funny, my wife was just saying the other day that I sit in that recliner so much I'm going to leave a permanent dent in it. It's a La-Z-Boy, you know. We bought it at that big furniture clearance center out on Route 47. The one with the giant inflatable gorilla on the roof. I always wondered how they keep those things inflated when the wind picks up. Remember that terrible storm we had back in... oh, must have been '93? The wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It blew the neighbor's oak tree right onto our garage roof. Took out the gutters and smashed my wife's prize rose bushes. She was so upset. She used to order these special hybrid tea roses from a catalog in England. The catalog had these glossy pages that smelled like ink and perfume... \n\nBut, uh, sleeping. No, I just sit there. I close my eyes sometimes, but I'm not really resting. I just can't seem to do anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving slowly as well, not just your body?\n\nPatient: Thoughts... yes, they feel like they're stuck in mud. I can’t keep the numbers straight for the senior center budgets anymore. I used to do all their accounting, you know. We kept everything in this big, heavy ledger... it had a dark green cover with gold lettering on the spine. We bought it at the old stationery store on Main Street before it closed down. Must have been '05 or '06. The fellow who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these clip-on bowties. He had a whole collection of them, polka dots, stripes, little anchors... he told me once that tying a real bowtie was a young man's game. His wife was a lovely woman. She made the absolute best rhubarb pie for the Methodist church bake sales every spring. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, because the leaves are poisonous if you're not careful. You have to know exactly when to harvest the stalks... \n\nBut, uh... my mind. Yes. It's just not working right. I'll sit at the kitchen table and just stare at a bill or the calendar for half an hour. I look at the paper and I just don't understand what I'm supposed to do with it. It all just feels completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worthlessness is interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Worthless... yes. I suppose that's the word. I just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I sit in that chair and I just keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With him, you know. And with the whole family. Like that time we were trying to fix the front porch steps. We bought this treated lumber from the old hardware store down the road... Miller's Hardware, it was called. Old man Miller ran it until he passed away in, oh, must have been '88. He had this big, beautiful brass cash register. The kind with the heavy keys you had to really push down hard, and it would make this loud bell sound when the drawer popped open. You could hear it clear across the store. He used to keep a glass jar of those hard butterscotch candies on the counter. The ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane that always got stuck to the candy if it was too warm out. My kids used to love those when they were little... \n\nBut, I... I was too hard on him that day. About the steps. I was always too hard on him, and everyone else. I just keep replaying all my mistakes, every single one, over and over in my head. I'm just no good to anyone now. I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. I really do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep... well, yes, I suppose. I wake up so early now. Usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. The house is so quiet then. It reminds me of when I used to work the early shift at the bottling plant back in the sixties. We bottled root beer, mostly. The glass bottles would come down this conveyor belt, clinking together. It was a beautiful sound, really, like a strange kind of music. We used real cane sugar back then, none of this high-fructose corn syrup nonsense. You could smell the sassafras and vanilla the minute you walked in the door. The foreman, a big burly fellow named Frank, he had a nose like a bloodhound. He could tell if the syrup mix was off by a fraction of an ounce just by sniffing the air. He drove this beautiful powder-blue Studebaker. Kept it polished so bright you could see your reflection in the hubcaps. I always wanted a Studebaker, but my wife said they weren't practical for a growing family...\n\nBut, yes, sleeping. I don't sleep much at all anymore. I just lie there in the dark after I wake up, staring at the ceiling, waiting for the sun to come up. It's just exhausting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I can’t keep the numbers straight for the senior center budgets anymore. I used to do all their accounting, you know. We kept everything in this big, heavy ledger... it had a dark green cover with gold lettering on the spine. We bought it at the old stationery store on Main Street before it closed down. Must have been '05 or '06. The fellow who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these clip-on bowties. He had a whole collection of them, polka dots, stripes, little anchors... he told me once that tying a real bowtie was a young man's game. His wife was a lovely woman. Like that time we were trying to fix the front porch steps. We bought this treated lumber from the old hardware store down the road... Miller's Hardware, it was called. Old man Miller ran it until he passed away in, oh, must have been '88. He had this big, beautiful brass cash register. The kind with the heavy keys you had to really push down hard, and it would make this loud bell sound when the drawer popped open. You could hear it clear across the store. He used to keep a glass jar of those hard butterscotch candies on the counter. The ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane that always got stuck to the candy if it was too warm out. My kids used to love those when they were little... \n\nBut, I... I was too hard on him that day. About the steps. I was always too hard on him, and everyone else. I just keep replaying all my mistakes, every single one, over and over in my head. I'm just no good to anyone now. I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. I really do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep... well, yes, I suppose. I wake up so early now. Usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. The house is so quiet then. It reminds me of when I used to work the early shift at the bottling plant back in the sixties. We bottled root beer, mostly. The glass bottles would come down this conveyor belt, clinking together. It was a beautiful sound, really, like a strange kind of music. We used real cane sugar back then, none of this high-fructose corn syrup nonsense. You could smell the sassafras and vanilla the minute you walked in the door. The foreman, a big burly fellow named Frank, he had a nose like a bloodhound. He could tell if the syrup mix was off by a fraction of an ounce just by sniffing the air. He drove this beautiful powder-blue Studebaker. Kept it polished so bright you could see your reflection in the hubcaps. I always wanted a Studebaker, but my wife said they weren't practical for a growing family...\n\nBut, yes, sleeping. I don't sleep much at all anymore. I just lie there in the dark after I wake up, staring at the ceiling, waiting for the sun to come up. It's just exhausting.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are present every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Every day? Yes... yes, every single day. The moment I open my eyes, it's right there waiting for me. I just... I keep going over every little thing I did wrong with him, you know? And with the whole family. It's like a film strip playing in my head. We used to have one of those old Bell & Howell projectors. The kind you had to thread the film through manually, over the little sprockets. If you didn't get the loops exactly right, the film would jitter and burn. You could smell it burning... that sharp, chemical smell. My uncle Charlie brought it back from the war, I think. Or maybe he bought it at a pawn shop in Chicago. He was always going to Chicago for business. He sold encyclopedias, door-to-door. The World Book, with the beautiful gold edging on the pages. He had this leather briefcase that squeaked when he walked. He said a good salesman always wore out his shoes before his suit... 

But, uh, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I just sit here and I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. Every day it's the same. I just deserve it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... what brings me in is I just keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I didn’t wake up. My wife, she's been so busy lately, bless her. She's putting together this big scrapbook for the family. Lots of old photos. We had to go buy special acid-free paper for it down at that craft place on Main Street. \n\nYou know, that building used to be a hardware store back in the eighties. I bought my favorite set of wrenches there. You just can't find tools made like that anymore, all heavy steel. Everything is plastic now... but, uh, what was I saying? \n\nAnyway. Lately I’ve just been saying I don’t need to see another winter. The winters just... well. I remember the blizzard of '78, now that was a real winter. We were snowed in for a week straight, had to play cards by candlelight...\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or taking your own life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just trying to say... I don't know about actually doing something to myself, but I just keep thinking they'd all be so much better off if I just didn't wake up. I really mean it when I tell them I don't need to see another winter. \n\nThe cold just... it just gets right into your joints anyway. We used to have this old cast-iron radiator in the house I grew up in. You'd stand next to it and it would practically burn your leg if you weren't careful. My mother used to dry our mittens on it after we'd been out sledding. She knitted them herself, you know. Real thick wool. You can't find wool like that anymore, everything you buy at the store now is synthetic. Makes your hands sweat but doesn't actually keep them warm. I bought a pair of gloves last year at the department store, the one out by the new highway bypass... they fell apart in two weeks. Just terrible stitching.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe your mood most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: My mood? Oh, it's just... heavy. Down, I suppose. Most days I just sit in my chair by the window and watch the cars go by. It's a nice chair, really. Leather. I bought it back in 1995 from that furniture place that used to be on the corner of Elm and 4th. They tore it down a few years ago to build a bank. Like we need another bank in this town. \n\nI remember the salesman, a young fellow named Jimmy. He had this terrible comb-over, bless his heart, but he knew his upholstery. He told me that leather would outlast me, and I suppose he was right. I used to read the morning paper in that chair every single day. The paper used to be so thick, you know? Full of real news. Now it's just a few pages of advertisements and they charge you twice as much for it. I stopped getting it delivered last spring. The boy who threw it always landed it in the rosebushes anyway. My wife's prize-winning roses... she won a blue ribbon for them at the county fair back in '02...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? No... no, I can't say that I do. I used to look forward to so much, you know. Playing bridge, tending to the garden, helping out with the cooking down at the church... oh, and going to the Cubs games. I used to just love the Cubs. We'd take the train right up to Wrigley. \n\nYou know, they don't make hot dogs like they used to at that ballpark. Used to be real beef with a snappy casing. Now it's just mush. And the mustard! I don't know why they changed the mustard. It used to be this bright yellow, tangy stuff. My buddy Frank, he'd always get that mustard all over his shirt. Frank passed away back in '08... or was it '09? He had this old Ford Taurus, ran like an absolute top until the transmission finally went out on him right in the middle of the intersection at Pine and 5th. The tow truck took three hours to get there...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh. Right. No, nowadays I just sit in that leather chair I told you about. I can't even make myself care about the Cubs or the garden or any of it anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you do sit in your chair and watch the cars go by?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no pleasure. It's just... something to do, I guess. Watching them go by. You see a lot of the same cars every day at the same time. There's this one fellow who drives a blue Chevy pickup, must be a late 90s model. He goes past every afternoon right at 3:15. \n\nYou know, they built those trucks tough back then. My brother-in-law, Artie, he had a Chevy. Drove it all the way to Yellowstone and back in the summer of '98. Said it never gave him a lick of trouble the whole way, not even over the mountains. The mountains are beautiful out there, you know. We took a trip out west when the kids were still small. Stopped at Mount Rushmore. It's smaller than you think it's going to be, looking at the pictures. And the gift shop... well, they just rob you blind in those places. Five dollars for a little plastic keychain. I told my son, I said, 'We're not paying five dollars for a piece of plastic.' He threw an absolute fit, right there in the store. Kids today, they don't understand the value of a dollar. \n\nBut... no. Sitting in the chair isn't pleasurable. It's just... sitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed. I don't step on the scale much anymore. We used to have this old mechanical scale in the bathroom, the kind with the little dial that spins around. My wife bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, it must have been '85. It was green. Avocado green, they called it back then. Everything in the 70s and 80s was avocado green or harvest gold. Our whole kitchen was harvest gold, right down to the toaster. \n\nI remember when the toaster finally gave out. It was a Sunday morning, and I was trying to make some rye toast to go with my eggs. Just popped right and sparked, nearly scared the cat half to death. We had this big orange tabby named Marmalade. Sweetest cat you ever met, but dumb as a box of rocks. He used to try and catch the dust motes in the sunlight... \n\nBut, uh, no... I don't think I've lost much weight. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh... yes. Yes, I do. All the time now. I just sit in that chair and I keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With my son, with my family... I feel like I'm just no good to anybody anymore. I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me, I really do. \n\nI was thinking about it just the other day, how I used to discipline the boy. We had this old leather belt, hung right on the back of the pantry door. The pantry had these squeaky hinges, drove my wife absolutely up the wall. I always told her I'd get around to oiling them, but you know how it is. You get busy with work, and the yard needs mowing, and the gutters need cleaning out. I used to clean my own gutters right up until I turned 70. Had this aluminum ladder, heavy as sin. Bought it at the Sears hardware store that used to be over by the old mall. \n\nThey tore that mall down, you know. Put up one of those big outdoor shopping centers. It's just not the same. In the wintertime, you freeze half to death just trying to walk from the shoe store to the food court. The old mall had this fountain right in the middle, and people would throw pennies in it. I wonder what they did with all those pennies when they demolished the place. Probably thousands of dollars just sitting in the bottom of that fountain...\n\nBut, yes. The guilt. It's heavy. I just... I'm just no good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, my wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow these days. And speaking of snow, boy, we used to get real winters back in the day. You remember the blizzard of '78? Now that was deep snow. We were snowed in for three solid days. I had this big two-stage Toro snowblower, heavy red metal housing. They don't make them like that anymore, everything is cheap plastic. You hit one little chunk of ice and the sheer pin snaps right in half. I used to buy those pins by the dozen down at Miller's Hardware. Bob Miller ran the place. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost it in Korea, or maybe it was a hunting accident, I can't quite recall. I always wondered if he took it out at night and put it in a glass of water on the nightstand, the way my grandfather used to do with his false teeth. Granddad had this awful set of dentures that clicked every time he chewed his food... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh. Yes. It just takes me so long now. Even just getting dressed, or crossing the kitchen... it takes so long that I usually just give up before I’ve done much of anything. I just sit back down in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find any activities that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No... no, not anymore. I used to. I used to love playing bridge with the Hendersons on Tuesday nights. We had this folding card table, covered in green felt. Real felt, mind you, not that cheap synthetic stuff they use now that pills up after two games. I bought it at a sporting goods store downtown that went out of business back in '92. The owner, fellow named Arthur, he used to play minor league baseball. Had this incredible collection of signed baseballs in a glass case right by the register. I always meant to ask him if he ever played against anyone famous, but you know how it is, you just never get around to asking the questions you want to ask. \n\nLike with my tomatoes. I used to love getting out in the garden. I'd grow these big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. The trick is to put crushed eggshells in the soil, for the calcium. My mother taught me that. She used to save all her eggshells in this little ceramic chicken on the windowsill. It had a chipped beak from when my brother knocked it over with a broom handle trying to swat a fly. He was always doing things like that, my brother. Clumsy as a newborn calf. He went into accounting, which was probably for the best, sitting at a desk where he couldn't break too much. \n\nI used to cook for the church potlucks, too, and go down to Wrigley Field for the Cubs games... but now? Now I just sit in my chair. I just sit there and I can't make myself care about any of it. None of it matters anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any pleasure or enjoyment from the things you do?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Oh... no. I don't think I've felt that in a long time. Like I was just saying about the bridge games, and the garden, and the cooking for the church... and those Cubs games. I used to get such a kick out of going down to Wrigley. The smell of the roasted peanuts, you know? They used to sell them in these little brown paper bags. The bags always had grease stains on the bottom. My father used to say that's how you knew they were good. He worked at a paper mill up in Wisconsin for thirty years. The smell of that mill was something awful, like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. We lived about two miles downwind, and on humid days, you couldn't even hang the laundry out to dry because the smell would get right into the bedsheets. My mother tried using lavender water in the wash, but it just made it smell like flowers and rotten eggs. She bought the lavender from this little apothecary shop downtown... \n\nBut, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I could get myself to go to a game or play cards, I wouldn't feel anything. I just sit in my chair, like I said. I just can't make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Hmm... no, I haven't really noticed much change. My wife usually keeps track of that sort of thing. She bought this new digital scale for the bathroom. It talks to you, can you believe that? You step on it and this robotic voice announces your weight to the whole house. I told her I liked the old dial one better, the one with the little red needle. We had that scale since we moved into the house in '68. We bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place for everything. You could buy a whole house from their catalog back in the day, they'd ship it to you on a train in pieces. My uncle Arthur built one of those kit houses out near Peoria. Took him two summers to finish it. He was a carpenter, lost his left index finger to a table saw when he was twenty-five... \n\nBut, uh, weight. No, I don't think it's changed. I don't eat much anymore anyway, but my clothes seem to fit about the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay still or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Slow motion... yes. My wife says I move like I'm walking through deep snow. Every little thing, even just getting dressed in the morning, it takes me so long. I usually just give up before I've done much of anything. Crossing the kitchen feels like a marathon. We put down that new linoleum in the kitchen back in... oh, must have been '98. It has this pattern that looks like little blue cobblestones. The fellow who installed it, he left his thermos on the counter. One of those heavy, metal Stanley thermoses. The green ones, you know? They don't make them like that anymore. Now they're all plastic and they don't keep the coffee hot for more than an hour. My father-in-law used to take a Stanley thermos with him to the railyard every single day. He worked for the Union Pacific. He used to tell these stories about the trains, the big diesel engines... he said the noise would shake your bones right inside your skin... \n\nBut, yes, everything just feels so slow. I can't seem to get my body to move the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleeping... well, I suppose I spend a lot of time in the chair. But sleep? No, I don't think I'm actually sleeping more. It's funny, my wife was just saying the other day that I sit in that recliner so much I'm going to leave a permanent dent in it. It's a La-Z-Boy, you know. We bought it at that big furniture clearance center out on Route 47. The one with the giant inflatable gorilla on the roof. I always wondered how they keep those things inflated when the wind picks up. Remember that terrible storm we had back in... oh, must have been '93? The wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It blew the neighbor's oak tree right onto our garage roof. Took out the gutters and smashed my wife's prize rose bushes. She was so upset. She used to order these special hybrid tea roses from a catalog in England. The catalog had these glossy pages that smelled like ink and perfume... \n\nBut, uh, sleeping. No, I just sit there. I close my eyes sometimes, but I'm not really resting. I just can't seem to do anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving slowly as well, not just your body?\n\nPatient: Thoughts... yes, they feel like they're stuck in mud. I can’t keep the numbers straight for the senior center budgets anymore. I used to do all their accounting, you know. We kept everything in this big, heavy ledger... it had a dark green cover with gold lettering on the spine. We bought it at the old stationery store on Main Street before it closed down. Must have been '05 or '06. The fellow who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these clip-on bowties. He had a whole collection of them, polka dots, stripes, little anchors... he told me once that tying a real bowtie was a young man's game. His wife was a lovely woman. She made the absolute best rhubarb pie for the Methodist church bake sales every spring. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, because the leaves are poisonous if you're not careful. You have to know exactly when to harvest the stalks... \n\nBut, uh... my mind. Yes. It's just not working right. I'll sit at the kitchen table and just stare at a bill or the calendar for half an hour. I look at the paper and I just don't understand what I'm supposed to do with it. It all just feels completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worthlessness is interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Worthless... yes. I suppose that's the word. I just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I sit in that chair and I just keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With him, you know. And with the whole family. Like that time we were trying to fix the front porch steps. We bought this treated lumber from the old hardware store down the road... Miller's Hardware, it was called. Old man Miller ran it until he passed away in, oh, must have been '88. He had this big, beautiful brass cash register. The kind with the heavy keys you had to really push down hard, and it would make this loud bell sound when the drawer popped open. You could hear it clear across the store. He used to keep a glass jar of those hard butterscotch candies on the counter. The ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane that always got stuck to the candy if it was too warm out. My kids used to love those when they were little... \n\nBut, I... I was too hard on him that day. About the steps. I was always too hard on him, and everyone else. I just keep replaying all my mistakes, every single one, over and over in my head. I'm just no good to anyone now. I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. I really do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep... well, yes, I suppose. I wake up so early now. Usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. The house is so quiet then. It reminds me of when I used to work the early shift at the bottling plant back in the sixties. We bottled root beer, mostly. The glass bottles would come down this conveyor belt, clinking together. It was a beautiful sound, really, like a strange kind of music. We used real cane sugar back then, none of this high-fructose corn syrup nonsense. You could smell the sassafras and vanilla the minute you walked in the door. The foreman, a big burly fellow named Frank, he had a nose like a bloodhound. He could tell if the syrup mix was off by a fraction of an ounce just by sniffing the air. He drove this beautiful powder-blue Studebaker. Kept it polished so bright you could see your reflection in the hubcaps. I always wanted a Studebaker, but my wife said they weren't practical for a growing family...\n\nBut, yes, sleeping. I don't sleep much at all anymore. I just lie there in the dark after I wake up, staring at the ceiling, waiting for the sun to come up. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? Yes... yes, every single day. The moment I open my eyes, it's right there waiting for me. I just... I keep going over every little thing I did wrong with him, you know? And with the whole family. It's like a film strip playing in my head. We used to have one of those old Bell & Howell projectors. The kind you had to thread the film through manually, over the little sprockets. If you didn't get the loops exactly right, the film would jitter and burn. You could smell it burning... that sharp, chemical smell. My uncle Charlie brought it back from the war, I think. Or maybe he bought it at a pawn shop in Chicago. He was always going to Chicago for business. He sold encyclopedias, door-to-door. The World Book, with the beautiful gold edging on the pages. He had this leather briefcase that squeaked when he walked. He said a good salesman always wore out his shoes before his suit... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I just sit here and I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. Every day it's the same. I just deserve it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I suppose that's the word. I just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I sit in that chair and I just keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With him, you know. And with the whole family. Like that time we were trying to fix the front porch steps. We bought this treated lumber from the old hardware store down the road... Miller's Hardware, it was called. Old man Miller ran it until he passed away in, oh, must have been '88. He had this big, beautiful brass cash register. The kind with the heavy keys you had to really push down hard, and it would make this loud bell sound when the drawer popped open. You could hear it clear across the store. He used to keep a glass jar of those hard butterscotch candies on the counter. The ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane that always got stuck to the candy if it was too warm out. My kids used to love those when they were little... \n\nBut, I... I was too hard on him that day. About the steps. I was always too hard on him, and everyone else. I just keep replaying all my mistakes, every single one, over and over in my head. I'm just no good to anyone now. I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. I really do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep... well, yes, I suppose. I wake up so early now. Usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. The house is so quiet then. It reminds me of when I used to work the early shift at the bottling plant back in the sixties. We bottled root beer, mostly. The glass bottles would come down this conveyor belt, clinking together. It was a beautiful sound, really, like a strange kind of music. We used real cane sugar back then, none of this high-fructose corn syrup nonsense. You could smell the sassafras and vanilla the minute you walked in the door. The foreman, a big burly fellow named Frank, he had a nose like a bloodhound. He could tell if the syrup mix was off by a fraction of an ounce just by sniffing the air. He drove this beautiful powder-blue Studebaker. Kept it polished so bright you could see your reflection in the hubcaps. I always wanted a Studebaker, but my wife said they weren't practical for a growing family...\n\nBut, yes, sleeping. I don't sleep much at all anymore. I just lie there in the dark after I wake up, staring at the ceiling, waiting for the sun to come up. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? Yes... yes, every single day. The moment I open my eyes, it's right there waiting for me. I just... I keep going over every little thing I did wrong with him, you know? And with the whole family. It's like a film strip playing in my head. We used to have one of those old Bell & Howell projectors. The kind you had to thread the film through manually, over the little sprockets. If you didn't get the loops exactly right, the film would jitter and burn. You could smell it burning... that sharp, chemical smell. My uncle Charlie brought it back from the war, I think. Or maybe he bought it at a pawn shop in Chicago. He was always going to Chicago for business. He sold encyclopedias, door-to-door. The World Book, with the beautiful gold edging on the pages. He had this leather briefcase that squeaked when he walked. He said a good salesman always wore out his shoes before his suit... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I just sit here and I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. Every day it's the same. I just deserve it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Work... taking care of things... no, I can't do any of it anymore. I've stopped being dependable, completely. I used to volunteer down at the senior center, you know. Setting up the chairs for the Tuesday bingo. We had these heavy metal folding chairs, the kind with the padded vinyl seats that always cracked right down the middle after a few years. They were a terrible mustard yellow color. The director, Mrs. Higgins, she bought them at a surplus auction from the old elementary school before they tore it down in '95. They built that new school out by the highway, the one with the big glass windows that cost the taxpayers an arm and a leg. Mrs. Higgins was a penny pincher, though. She used to save the little plastic tabs from bread bags to use as bingo markers if we ran short. She had a whole coffee can full of them... Folgers, I think it was. 

But I... I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't. And at home, it's worse. My wife, she's had to take over everything. The bills, the calendar, making all the decisions I used to handle. I just sit there. I think my family and Pastor Thomas got really scared by the things I was saying. About the punishment, you know. That's why they pushed me to get urgent help. I'm just a burden to everyone now.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... what brings me in is I just keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I didn’t wake up. My wife, she's been so busy lately, bless her. She's putting together this big scrapbook for the family. Lots of old photos. We had to go buy special acid-free paper for it down at that craft place on Main Street. \n\nYou know, that building used to be a hardware store back in the eighties. I bought my favorite set of wrenches there. You just can't find tools made like that anymore, all heavy steel. Everything is plastic now... but, uh, what was I saying? \n\nAnyway. Lately I’ve just been saying I don’t need to see another winter. The winters just... well. I remember the blizzard of '78, now that was a real winter. We were snowed in for a week straight, had to play cards by candlelight...\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of actually harming yourself or taking your own life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just trying to say... I don't know about actually doing something to myself, but I just keep thinking they'd all be so much better off if I just didn't wake up. I really mean it when I tell them I don't need to see another winter. \n\nThe cold just... it just gets right into your joints anyway. We used to have this old cast-iron radiator in the house I grew up in. You'd stand next to it and it would practically burn your leg if you weren't careful. My mother used to dry our mittens on it after we'd been out sledding. She knitted them herself, you know. Real thick wool. You can't find wool like that anymore, everything you buy at the store now is synthetic. Makes your hands sweat but doesn't actually keep them warm. I bought a pair of gloves last year at the department store, the one out by the new highway bypass... they fell apart in two weeks. Just terrible stitching.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe your mood most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: My mood? Oh, it's just... heavy. Down, I suppose. Most days I just sit in my chair by the window and watch the cars go by. It's a nice chair, really. Leather. I bought it back in 1995 from that furniture place that used to be on the corner of Elm and 4th. They tore it down a few years ago to build a bank. Like we need another bank in this town. \n\nI remember the salesman, a young fellow named Jimmy. He had this terrible comb-over, bless his heart, but he knew his upholstery. He told me that leather would outlast me, and I suppose he was right. I used to read the morning paper in that chair every single day. The paper used to be so thick, you know? Full of real news. Now it's just a few pages of advertisements and they charge you twice as much for it. I stopped getting it delivered last spring. The boy who threw it always landed it in the rosebushes anyway. My wife's prize-winning roses... she won a blue ribbon for them at the county fair back in '02...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? No... no, I can't say that I do. I used to look forward to so much, you know. Playing bridge, tending to the garden, helping out with the cooking down at the church... oh, and going to the Cubs games. I used to just love the Cubs. We'd take the train right up to Wrigley. \n\nYou know, they don't make hot dogs like they used to at that ballpark. Used to be real beef with a snappy casing. Now it's just mush. And the mustard! I don't know why they changed the mustard. It used to be this bright yellow, tangy stuff. My buddy Frank, he'd always get that mustard all over his shirt. Frank passed away back in '08... or was it '09? He had this old Ford Taurus, ran like an absolute top until the transmission finally went out on him right in the middle of the intersection at Pine and 5th. The tow truck took three hours to get there...\n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh. Right. No, nowadays I just sit in that leather chair I told you about. I can't even make myself care about the Cubs or the garden or any of it anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you do sit in your chair and watch the cars go by?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no pleasure. It's just... something to do, I guess. Watching them go by. You see a lot of the same cars every day at the same time. There's this one fellow who drives a blue Chevy pickup, must be a late 90s model. He goes past every afternoon right at 3:15. \n\nYou know, they built those trucks tough back then. My brother-in-law, Artie, he had a Chevy. Drove it all the way to Yellowstone and back in the summer of '98. Said it never gave him a lick of trouble the whole way, not even over the mountains. The mountains are beautiful out there, you know. We took a trip out west when the kids were still small. Stopped at Mount Rushmore. It's smaller than you think it's going to be, looking at the pictures. And the gift shop... well, they just rob you blind in those places. Five dollars for a little plastic keychain. I told my son, I said, 'We're not paying five dollars for a piece of plastic.' He threw an absolute fit, right there in the store. Kids today, they don't understand the value of a dollar. \n\nBut... no. Sitting in the chair isn't pleasurable. It's just... sitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed. I don't step on the scale much anymore. We used to have this old mechanical scale in the bathroom, the kind with the little dial that spins around. My wife bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, it must have been '85. It was green. Avocado green, they called it back then. Everything in the 70s and 80s was avocado green or harvest gold. Our whole kitchen was harvest gold, right down to the toaster. \n\nI remember when the toaster finally gave out. It was a Sunday morning, and I was trying to make some rye toast to go with my eggs. Just popped right and sparked, nearly scared the cat half to death. We had this big orange tabby named Marmalade. Sweetest cat you ever met, but dumb as a box of rocks. He used to try and catch the dust motes in the sunlight... \n\nBut, uh, no... I don't think I've lost much weight. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh... yes. Yes, I do. All the time now. I just sit in that chair and I keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With my son, with my family... I feel like I'm just no good to anybody anymore. I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me, I really do. \n\nI was thinking about it just the other day, how I used to discipline the boy. We had this old leather belt, hung right on the back of the pantry door. The pantry had these squeaky hinges, drove my wife absolutely up the wall. I always told her I'd get around to oiling them, but you know how it is. You get busy with work, and the yard needs mowing, and the gutters need cleaning out. I used to clean my own gutters right up until I turned 70. Had this aluminum ladder, heavy as sin. Bought it at the Sears hardware store that used to be over by the old mall. \n\nThey tore that mall down, you know. Put up one of those big outdoor shopping centers. It's just not the same. In the wintertime, you freeze half to death just trying to walk from the shoe store to the food court. The old mall had this fountain right in the middle, and people would throw pennies in it. I wonder what they did with all those pennies when they demolished the place. Probably thousands of dollars just sitting in the bottom of that fountain...\n\nBut, yes. The guilt. It's heavy. I just... I'm just no good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, my wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow these days. And speaking of snow, boy, we used to get real winters back in the day. You remember the blizzard of '78? Now that was deep snow. We were snowed in for three solid days. I had this big two-stage Toro snowblower, heavy red metal housing. They don't make them like that anymore, everything is cheap plastic. You hit one little chunk of ice and the sheer pin snaps right in half. I used to buy those pins by the dozen down at Miller's Hardware. Bob Miller ran the place. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost it in Korea, or maybe it was a hunting accident, I can't quite recall. I always wondered if he took it out at night and put it in a glass of water on the nightstand, the way my grandfather used to do with his false teeth. Granddad had this awful set of dentures that clicked every time he chewed his food... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh. Yes. It just takes me so long now. Even just getting dressed, or crossing the kitchen... it takes so long that I usually just give up before I’ve done much of anything. I just sit back down in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find any activities that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No... no, not anymore. I used to. I used to love playing bridge with the Hendersons on Tuesday nights. We had this folding card table, covered in green felt. Real felt, mind you, not that cheap synthetic stuff they use now that pills up after two games. I bought it at a sporting goods store downtown that went out of business back in '92. The owner, fellow named Arthur, he used to play minor league baseball. Had this incredible collection of signed baseballs in a glass case right by the register. I always meant to ask him if he ever played against anyone famous, but you know how it is, you just never get around to asking the questions you want to ask. \n\nLike with my tomatoes. I used to love getting out in the garden. I'd grow these big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. The trick is to put crushed eggshells in the soil, for the calcium. My mother taught me that. She used to save all her eggshells in this little ceramic chicken on the windowsill. It had a chipped beak from when my brother knocked it over with a broom handle trying to swat a fly. He was always doing things like that, my brother. Clumsy as a newborn calf. He went into accounting, which was probably for the best, sitting at a desk where he couldn't break too much. \n\nI used to cook for the church potlucks, too, and go down to Wrigley Field for the Cubs games... but now? Now I just sit in my chair. I just sit there and I can't make myself care about any of it. None of it matters anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any pleasure or enjoyment from the things you do?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Oh... no. I don't think I've felt that in a long time. Like I was just saying about the bridge games, and the garden, and the cooking for the church... and those Cubs games. I used to get such a kick out of going down to Wrigley. The smell of the roasted peanuts, you know? They used to sell them in these little brown paper bags. The bags always had grease stains on the bottom. My father used to say that's how you knew they were good. He worked at a paper mill up in Wisconsin for thirty years. The smell of that mill was something awful, like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. We lived about two miles downwind, and on humid days, you couldn't even hang the laundry out to dry because the smell would get right into the bedsheets. My mother tried using lavender water in the wash, but it just made it smell like flowers and rotten eggs. She bought the lavender from this little apothecary shop downtown... \n\nBut, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I could get myself to go to a game or play cards, I wouldn't feel anything. I just sit in my chair, like I said. I just can't make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Hmm... no, I haven't really noticed much change. My wife usually keeps track of that sort of thing. She bought this new digital scale for the bathroom. It talks to you, can you believe that? You step on it and this robotic voice announces your weight to the whole house. I told her I liked the old dial one better, the one with the little red needle. We had that scale since we moved into the house in '68. We bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place for everything. You could buy a whole house from their catalog back in the day, they'd ship it to you on a train in pieces. My uncle Arthur built one of those kit houses out near Peoria. Took him two summers to finish it. He was a carpenter, lost his left index finger to a table saw when he was twenty-five... \n\nBut, uh, weight. No, I don't think it's changed. I don't eat much anymore anyway, but my clothes seem to fit about the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay still or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Slow motion... yes. My wife says I move like I'm walking through deep snow. Every little thing, even just getting dressed in the morning, it takes me so long. I usually just give up before I've done much of anything. Crossing the kitchen feels like a marathon. We put down that new linoleum in the kitchen back in... oh, must have been '98. It has this pattern that looks like little blue cobblestones. The fellow who installed it, he left his thermos on the counter. One of those heavy, metal Stanley thermoses. The green ones, you know? They don't make them like that anymore. Now they're all plastic and they don't keep the coffee hot for more than an hour. My father-in-law used to take a Stanley thermos with him to the railyard every single day. He worked for the Union Pacific. He used to tell these stories about the trains, the big diesel engines... he said the noise would shake your bones right inside your skin... \n\nBut, yes, everything just feels so slow. I can't seem to get my body to move the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleeping... well, I suppose I spend a lot of time in the chair. But sleep? No, I don't think I'm actually sleeping more. It's funny, my wife was just saying the other day that I sit in that recliner so much I'm going to leave a permanent dent in it. It's a La-Z-Boy, you know. We bought it at that big furniture clearance center out on Route 47. The one with the giant inflatable gorilla on the roof. I always wondered how they keep those things inflated when the wind picks up. Remember that terrible storm we had back in... oh, must have been '93? The wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It blew the neighbor's oak tree right onto our garage roof. Took out the gutters and smashed my wife's prize rose bushes. She was so upset. She used to order these special hybrid tea roses from a catalog in England. The catalog had these glossy pages that smelled like ink and perfume... \n\nBut, uh, sleeping. No, I just sit there. I close my eyes sometimes, but I'm not really resting. I just can't seem to do anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are moving slowly as well, not just your body?\n\nPatient: Thoughts... yes, they feel like they're stuck in mud. I can’t keep the numbers straight for the senior center budgets anymore. I used to do all their accounting, you know. We kept everything in this big, heavy ledger... it had a dark green cover with gold lettering on the spine. We bought it at the old stationery store on Main Street before it closed down. Must have been '05 or '06. The fellow who ran the place, Mr. Henderson, he always wore these clip-on bowties. He had a whole collection of them, polka dots, stripes, little anchors... he told me once that tying a real bowtie was a young man's game. His wife was a lovely woman. She made the absolute best rhubarb pie for the Methodist church bake sales every spring. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, because the leaves are poisonous if you're not careful. You have to know exactly when to harvest the stalks... \n\nBut, uh... my mind. Yes. It's just not working right. I'll sit at the kitchen table and just stare at a bill or the calendar for half an hour. I look at the paper and I just don't understand what I'm supposed to do with it. It all just feels completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worthlessness is interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Worthless... yes. I suppose that's the word. I just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I sit in that chair and I just keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With him, you know. And with the whole family. Like that time we were trying to fix the front porch steps. We bought this treated lumber from the old hardware store down the road... Miller's Hardware, it was called. Old man Miller ran it until he passed away in, oh, must have been '88. He had this big, beautiful brass cash register. The kind with the heavy keys you had to really push down hard, and it would make this loud bell sound when the drawer popped open. You could hear it clear across the store. He used to keep a glass jar of those hard butterscotch candies on the counter. The ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane that always got stuck to the candy if it was too warm out. My kids used to love those when they were little... \n\nBut, I... I was too hard on him that day. About the steps. I was always too hard on him, and everyone else. I just keep replaying all my mistakes, every single one, over and over in my head. I'm just no good to anyone now. I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. I really do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping less than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep... well, yes, I suppose. I wake up so early now. Usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. The house is so quiet then. It reminds me of when I used to work the early shift at the bottling plant back in the sixties. We bottled root beer, mostly. The glass bottles would come down this conveyor belt, clinking together. It was a beautiful sound, really, like a strange kind of music. We used real cane sugar back then, none of this high-fructose corn syrup nonsense. You could smell the sassafras and vanilla the minute you walked in the door. The foreman, a big burly fellow named Frank, he had a nose like a bloodhound. He could tell if the syrup mix was off by a fraction of an ounce just by sniffing the air. He drove this beautiful powder-blue Studebaker. Kept it polished so bright you could see your reflection in the hubcaps. I always wanted a Studebaker, but my wife said they weren't practical for a growing family...\n\nBut, yes, sleeping. I don't sleep much at all anymore. I just lie there in the dark after I wake up, staring at the ceiling, waiting for the sun to come up. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are present every day?\n\nPatient: Every day? Yes... yes, every single day. The moment I open my eyes, it's right there waiting for me. I just... I keep going over every little thing I did wrong with him, you know? And with the whole family. It's like a film strip playing in my head. We used to have one of those old Bell & Howell projectors. The kind you had to thread the film through manually, over the little sprockets. If you didn't get the loops exactly right, the film would jitter and burn. You could smell it burning... that sharp, chemical smell. My uncle Charlie brought it back from the war, I think. Or maybe he bought it at a pawn shop in Chicago. He was always going to Chicago for business. He sold encyclopedias, door-to-door. The World Book, with the beautiful gold edging on the pages. He had this leather briefcase that squeaked when he walked. He said a good salesman always wore out his shoes before his suit... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's just... I'm no good to anybody anymore. I just sit here and I know I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me. Every day it's the same. I just deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Work... taking care of things... no, I can't do any of it anymore. I've stopped being dependable, completely. I used to volunteer down at the senior center, you know. Setting up the chairs for the Tuesday bingo. We had these heavy metal folding chairs, the kind with the padded vinyl seats that always cracked right down the middle after a few years. They were a terrible mustard yellow color. The director, Mrs. Higgins, she bought them at a surplus auction from the old elementary school before they tore it down in '95. They built that new school out by the highway, the one with the big glass windows that cost the taxpayers an arm and a leg. Mrs. Higgins was a penny pincher, though. She used to save the little plastic tabs from bread bags to use as bingo markers if we ran short. She had a whole coffee can full of them... Folgers, I think it was. \n\nBut I... I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't. And at home, it's worse. My wife, she's had to take over everything. The bills, the calendar, making all the decisions I used to handle. I just sit there. I think my family and Pastor Thomas got really scared by the things I was saying. About the punishment, you know. That's why they pushed me to get urgent help. I'm just a burden to everyone now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... what brings me in is I just keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I didn’t wake up. My wife, she's been so busy lately, bless her. She's putting together this big scrapbook for the family. Lots of old photos. We had to go buy special acid-free paper for it down at that craft place on Main Street. \n\nYou know, that building used to be a hardware store back in the eighties. We used to have this old cast-iron radiator in the house I grew up in. You'd stand next to it and it would practically burn your leg if you weren't careful. Drove it all the way to Yellowstone and back in the summer of '98. Said it never gave him a lick of trouble the whole way, not even over the mountains. The mountains are beautiful out there, you know. We took a trip out west when the kids were still small. Stopped at Mount Rushmore. It's smaller than you think it's going to be, looking at the pictures. And the gift shop... well, they just rob you blind in those places. Five dollars for a little plastic keychain. I told my son, I said, 'We're not paying five dollars for a piece of plastic.' He threw an absolute fit, right there in the store. Kids today, they don't understand the value of a dollar. \n\nBut... no. Sitting in the chair isn't pleasurable. It's just... sitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed. I don't step on the scale much anymore. We used to have this old mechanical scale in the bathroom, the kind with the little dial that spins around. My wife bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, it must have been '85. It was green. Avocado green, they called it back then. Everything in the 70s and 80s was avocado green or harvest gold. Our whole kitchen was harvest gold, right down to the toaster. \n\nI remember when the toaster finally gave out. It was a Sunday morning, and I was trying to make some rye toast to go with my eggs. Just popped right and sparked, nearly scared the cat half to death. We had this big orange tabby named Marmalade. Sweetest cat you ever met, but dumb as a box of rocks. He used to try and catch the dust motes in the sunlight... \n\nBut, uh, no... I don't think I've lost much weight. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh... yes. Yes, I do. All the time now. I just sit in that chair and I keep going over every little thing I did wrong. With my son, with my family... I feel like I'm just no good to anybody anymore. I deserve whatever punishment is coming to me, I really do. \n\nI was thinking about it just the other day, how I used to discipline the boy. We had this old leather belt, hung right on the back of the pantry door. The pantry had these squeaky hinges, drove my wife absolutely up the wall. I always told her I'd get around to oiling them, but you know how it is. You get busy with work, and the yard needs mowing, and the gutters need cleaning out. I used to clean my own gutters right up until I turned 70. Had this aluminum ladder, heavy as sin. Bought it at the Sears hardware store that used to be over by the old mall. \n\nThey tore that mall down, you know. Put up one of those big outdoor shopping centers. It's just not the same. In the wintertime, you freeze half to death just trying to walk from the shoe store to the food court. The old mall had this fountain right in the middle, and people would throw pennies in it. I wonder what they did with all those pennies when they demolished the place. Probably thousands of dollars just sitting in the bottom of that fountain...\n\nBut, yes. The guilt. It's heavy. I just... I'm just no good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, my wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow these days. And speaking of snow, boy, we used to get real winters back in the day. You remember the blizzard of '78? Now that was deep snow. We were snowed in for three solid days. I had this big two-stage Toro snowblower, heavy red metal housing. They don't make them like that anymore, everything is cheap plastic. You hit one little chunk of ice and the sheer pin snaps right in half. I used to buy those pins by the dozen down at Miller's Hardware. Bob Miller ran the place. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost it in Korea, or maybe it was a hunting accident, I can't quite recall. I always wondered if he took it out at night and put it in a glass of water on the nightstand, the way my grandfather used to do with his false teeth. Granddad had this awful set of dentures that clicked every time he chewed his food... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh. Yes. It just takes me so long now. Even just getting dressed, or crossing the kitchen... it takes so long that I usually just give up before I’ve done much of anything. I just sit back down in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find any activities that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No... no, not anymore. I used to. I used to love playing bridge with the Hendersons on Tuesday nights. We had this folding card table, covered in green felt. Real felt, mind you, not that cheap synthetic stuff they use now that pills up after two games. I bought it at a sporting goods store downtown that went out of business back in '92. The owner, fellow named Arthur, he used to play minor league baseball. Had this incredible collection of signed baseballs in a glass case right by the register. I always meant to ask him if he ever played against anyone famous, but you know how it is, you just never get around to asking the questions you want to ask. \n\nLike with my tomatoes. I used to love getting out in the garden. I'd grow these big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. The trick is to put crushed eggshells in the soil, for the calcium. My mother taught me that. We lived about two miles downwind, and on humid days, you couldn't even hang the laundry out to dry because the smell would get right into the bedsheets. I just can't make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Hmm... no, I haven't really noticed much change. You step on it and this robotic voice announces your weight to the whole house. I told her I liked the old dial one better, the one with the little red needle. We had that scale since we moved into the house in '68. We bought it at Sears. Sears used to be the place for everything. You could buy a whole house from their catalog back in the day, they'd ship it to you on a train in pieces. My uncle Arthur built one of those kit houses out near Peoria.
